# 3 Framework for Design and Performance Evaluation of Memory Using a Memristor

Jalpaben Pandya, Ramji Gupta, and Alpana Pandey

DOI: [10.1201/9781003483038-3](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003483038-3)

## 3.1 Introduction

The electronics sector is experiencing rapid growth due to the emergence of flexible and intelligent electronics. Flexible and smart electronics are distinguished by their elevated circuit complexity and substantial data storage capacity \[[1](#ch03.xhtml#r3_1), [2](#ch03.xhtml#r3_2), [3](#ch03.xhtml#r3_3), [4](#ch03.xhtml#r3_4)\] Historically, data storage has been achieved via scaling CMOS technology to keep in pace with Moore’s law. However, when attempting to scale processors below 22-nm CMOS technology, quantum effects arise, and the chips become more prone to process, voltage, and temperature (PVT) changes \[[5](#ch03.xhtml#r3_5),[6](#ch03.xhtml#r3_6)\]. This leads to a decline in the performance of the CMOS technology when it comes to storing large amounts of data while using less power and occupying a larger space. Therefore, it is necessary to construct nonvolatile memories employing the new memristor technology, which effectively addresses all the current problems associated with the CMOS technology \[[7](#ch03.xhtml#r3_7), [8](#ch03.xhtml#r3_8), [9](#ch03.xhtml#r3_9), [10](#ch03.xhtml#r3_10), [11](#ch03.xhtml#r3_11), [12](#ch03.xhtml#r3_12)\].

The research on memory design with memristors has focused on exploring the potential benefits of memristor-based memories, including its high density, low power consumption, and nonvolatile nature. Memristors, also known as memory resistors, are electronic devices with two terminals that control the electric current and store the charge last passed through it in a circuit \[[5](#ch03.xhtml#r3_5),[11](#ch03.xhtml#r3_11), [12](#ch03.xhtml#r3_12), [13](#ch03.xhtml#r3_13)\]. Memristor is a component which links flux and charge as given in equation (3.1) \[[11](#ch03.xhtml#r3_11),[14](#ch03.xhtml#r3_14)\].

*d*⌀ = *M**d**q*(3.1)

where

1.  ⌀ – flux
2.  *M* – memristance
3.  *q* – charge

This device was merely a conceptualization until it was physically fabricated in 2008. This first model of memristor consisted of two layers, namely TiO[2] layer (undoped) which is highly resistive and TiO[2 − *x*] layer (doped with oxygen vacancies) which is highly conductive in nature \[[7](#ch03.xhtml#r3_7),[13](#ch03.xhtml#r3_13)\] Platinum electrodes are connected on both the sides wherein the TiO[2] behaves as a negative terminal, whereas the TiO[2 − *x*] layer behaves as a positive terminal as illustrated in [Figure 3.1](#ch03.xhtml#f3_1) along with its symbol as shown in [Figure 3.2](#ch03.xhtml#f3_2).

![FIGURE 3.1 Vertical cross-sectional structure of HP model memristor where W is known as the state variable that corresponds to the \\text{TiO}\_{2 - x} region width and D symbolizes the memristor device width.](images/Fig03_01.jpg)

[FIGURE 3.1](#ch03.xhtml#f3_1b) Vertical cross-sectional structure of HP model memristor where W is known as the state variable that corresponds to the TiO[2 − *x*] region width and D symbolizes the memristor device width.

![FIGURE 3.2 Symbolic representation of a memristor \[7\].](images/Fig03_02.jpg)

[FIGURE 3.2](#ch03.xhtml#f3_2b) Symbolic representation of a memristor \[[7](#ch03.xhtml#r3_7)\].

If a positive supply is applied to the (TiO[2 − *x*]) layer and the negative supply to the TiO[2] layer, the positive oxygen ions that are present in the metallic layer experience a force of repulsion due to which these ions are pushed deep into the insulating layer of TiO[2]. This creates a boundary between the two materials shifting towards the insulating layer. Thus, the overall percentage of conducting TiO[2 − *x*] increases. Consequently, the overall memristor resistance decreases corresponding to an “ON” state.

In contrast, if a negative supply is given to the (TiO[2 − *x*]) layer and the positive supply is given to the TiO[2] layer, under this condition, the positively charged oxygen ions that are present in the insulator layer experience a force of attraction due to which it pulls the positively charged oxygen ions from the TiO[2] layer. This results in the boundary between the two materials shifting towards the conducting layer. Hence, the width of the insulating layer TiO[2] increases, thereby making the device less conductive \[[15](#ch03.xhtml#r3_15)\]. The resistance returns to its initial state, which corresponds to an “OFF” state. By applying voltage to the electrodes, which causes the resistive switching layer to undergo a resistance shift between the high-resistance state *R*[OFF] and the low-resistance state *R*[ON], the device’s resistance may be adjusted. The SET process and RESET process are the terms used to describe the transitions between high- and low-resistance states, respectively.

Even though memristors have well-defined mathematical formulas, simulating a circuit using them is difficult. The absence of mathematical models for simulating memristor circuits is the reason for this. Therefore, in an effort to mimic a memristor’s behaviour, the three fingerprints, namely “Bow Tied *V*–*I* curve, *V*–*I* Curve Area inversely related to frequency, and V–I curve attaining a single value at infinite frequency” need to be met \[[14](#ch03.xhtml#r3_14),[16](#ch03.xhtml#r3_16)\].

## 3.2 Memristor Models

The behaviour of the physical memristor developed was quite similar to the mathematical formulas suggested by Leon Chua \[[11](#ch03.xhtml#r3_11)\]. After the development of this model, numerous efforts have been undertaken to create an appropriate and uniform memristor model that can capture the accurate memristor behaviour suggested by Chua \[[11](#ch03.xhtml#r3_11)\]. The different memristor models available are as follows:

1.  Linear ionic drift model
2.  Nonlinear ionic drift model
3.  Yakopcic neuromorphic memristor model
4.  Simmons tunnel barrier model
5.  ThrEshold adaptive memristor model (TEAM)
6.  Voltage TEAM (V-TEAM) model
7.  Elwakil model for double-loop hysteresis
8.  Model for complementary resistive switches (CRS)

The linear ionic drift model \[[12](#ch03.xhtml#r3_12)\] provides basic equations for modelling an ideal memristor. Nevertheless, it is plagued by boundary effect and diminished nonlinearity. To address the boundary effect, several window functions have been suggested for memristor modelling, including Strukov, Joglekar, Biolek, Prodromakis, Piecewise, and Zha. Each of these functions has its own advantages and disadvantages. [Table 3.1](#ch03.xhtml#t3_1) shows various window functions together with their respective drawbacks.

[TABLE 3.1](#ch03.xhtml#t3_1b)Different Window Functions along with its Disadvantages

  
| Sr. No || Window || Window Function || Disadvantages |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1. || Strukov’s window function \[[12](#ch03.xhtml#r3_12)\] || $f\\left( w \\right) = \\frac{w\\left( 1 - w \\right)}{D^{2}}$*D* and *w* are the parameters as stated in [Figure 1.1](#ch01.xhtml#f1_1) || Boundary lock issue present and cannot capture nonlinear effects |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 2. || Joglekar’s window function \[[17](#ch03.xhtml#r3_17)\] || *f*(*x*) = 1 − (2*x*−1)^(2*p*)where *x* – internal state variable and $x = \\frac{w}{D}$ || Boundary lock issue present |
| 3. || Biolek’s window function \[[18](#ch03.xhtml#r3_18)\] || *f*(*x*) = 1 − (*x*−sgn(−*i*))^(2*p*)where *p* – positive integesgn(−*i*) – current through memristor || Discontinuities at the boundaries |
| 4. || Prodromakis’ window function \[[19](#ch03.xhtml#r3_19)\] || $f\\left( w \\right) = j\\left\\{ 1 - \\left\\lbrack \\left( \\frac{w}{D} - 0.5 \\right)^{2} + 0.75 \\right\\rbrack^{p} \\right\\}$where *p*, *j* – control parameters || At low frequencies, it fails to retain the pinched hysteresis loop |
| 5. || Piecewise window function \[[20](#ch03.xhtml#r3_20)\] || $\\begin{array}{l} {f\\left( x \\right) = \\left( 1 + \\left( \\frac{x - 0.25}{a} \\right)^{2b} \\right)^{- 1};\\quad} \\\\ {x\_{0} \\leq x \\leq 1 - x} \\\\ \\end{array}$$f\\left( x \\right) = kx\\left( 1 - x \\right);\\text{\\quad otherwise}$$\\text{where}x = \\frac{w}{D}$ || Needs careful parameter selection to guarantee precision |
| 6. || Zha’s window function \[[21](#ch03.xhtml#r3_21)\] || *f*(*x*) = *j*(1−{0.25\[*x*−sgn(−*i*)\]²+0.75}^(*p*))where *p*, *j* are the control parameters || Boundary lock issues present |
| 7. || Window function suggested by T. Anusudha \[[22](#ch03.xhtml#r3_22)\] || *f*(*x*) = *J*\[1−2(*x*³−*x*+1)^(*p*)\]where *x* – internal state variable*p* – positive integer*J* – control parameter || Needs careful parameter selection to guarantee precision |
| 8. || Window function suggested by Junrui \[[23](#ch03.xhtml#r3_23)\] || $f\\left( x \\right) = j\\left\\{ 1 - \\left\\lbrack \\begin{array}{l} {\\alpha x^{3} + a^{2}\\left\\lbrack x - \\text{stp}\\left( - i \\right) \\right\\rbrack^{2}} \\\\ {+ \\left( 1 - a^{2} \\right) + \\beta x^{2} + \\gamma x} \\\\ \\end{array} \\right\\rbrack^{p} \\right\\}$where*α*, *β*, *γ* – of nonlinear drift parameters*j* – control parameter*p* – positive integer || Complicated function |

In addition, other window functions have been developed to address the boundary lock problem by introducing nonlinearity and emulating the properties of an actual memristor device. Jeetendra Singh \[[24](#ch03.xhtml#r3_24)\] proposed a window function that considers both the linear and nonlinear dopant drift of dopants within a memristor. This function demonstrates a significant nonlinearity at the frontiers and effectively addresses other boundary concerns, making it valuable for nonlinear memristor models. Therefore, it is crucial that the window design incorporates the capability to introduce controlled nonlinearity, addresses border impact and boundary lock issues, and allows for scalability.

The nonlinear ionic drift model \[[25](#ch03.xhtml#r3_25)\] yields more precise outcomes in relation to memristive devices when compared to the linear ionic drift model. Nevertheless, it demonstrates asymmetrical switching behaviour without any threshold being taken into account. The Yakopcic neuromorphic memristor model \[[26](#ch03.xhtml#r3_26)\] enables the incorporation of memristors with threshold characteristics to construct intricate neuromorphic memristor-based systems \[[5](#ch03.xhtml#r3_5),[26](#ch03.xhtml#r3_26)\]. The Simmons tunnel barrier model \[[27](#ch03.xhtml#r3_27)\] is another memristor model created using SPICE software. This model accurately represents the device as a resistor coupled with an electron tunnel barrier. This model demonstrates symmetric switching without any explicit correlation between voltage and current and is a complex model. The threshold adaptive memristor model (TEAM) \[[28](#ch03.xhtml#r3_28)\] represents a device that adjusts its resistance based on threshold currents and changes in resistance when the input current surpasses a certain level. This model is characterized by its intricate nature and high level of precision, making it particularly valuable for applications involving switching or digital processes. The V-TEAM model \[[29](#ch03.xhtml#r3_29)\] is a developed model that considers the threshold voltage. The V-TEAM model demonstrates superior accuracy in comparison to the existing memristor models that are currently in use \[[29](#ch03.xhtml#r3_29),[30](#ch03.xhtml#r3_30)\]. Elwakil devised a model for double-loop hysteresis \[[31](#ch03.xhtml#r3_31)\] that offers representations for both current- and voltage-driven memristors that display asymmetrical switching behaviour. Memristor-based memory circuits are affected by sneak path currents, which significantly impair their performance \[[32](#ch03.xhtml#r3_32),[33](#ch03.xhtml#r3_33)\]. To prevent sneak path current in the circuit, the model for CRS \[[33](#ch03.xhtml#r3_33)\] is employed. This model consists of two memristive components that are coupled in an anti-serial arrangement. This model is effective in addressing the problems related to sneak path currents. However, when dealing with bigger memory array sizes, it experiences slight convergence challenges.

In theory, the behaviour of memristor devices can be accurately predicted using the stated memristor device models. However, there has been limited study conducted on memristor devices in real-world contexts, where their usage differs dramatically from theoretical studies \[[15](#ch03.xhtml#r3_15)\]. Insufficient attention is given to the interaction between memristive circuits and the real world when they are utilized in an uncontrolled context, leading to potentially inaccurate conclusions.

The previously described models only partially consider nonlinearity and kinetics, which impacts the device speed. However, for modelling accurate speed at the device level, the performance of memory cells built with memristors is compromised because design considerations for various parameters, such as leakage current, state drift, noise, device variability, temperature, and parasitic effects, are omitted \[[34](#ch03.xhtml#r3_34), [35](#ch03.xhtml#r3_35), [36](#ch03.xhtml#r3_36), [37](#ch03.xhtml#r3_37)\]. Additionally, other emulator models tailored for certain applications have been developed \[[34](#ch03.xhtml#r3_34), [35](#ch03.xhtml#r3_35), [36](#ch03.xhtml#r3_36), [37](#ch03.xhtml#r3_37), [38](#ch03.xhtml#r3_38), [39](#ch03.xhtml#r3_39), [40](#ch03.xhtml#r3_40), [41](#ch03.xhtml#r3_41), [42](#ch03.xhtml#r3_42), [43](#ch03.xhtml#r3_43)\] to mimic and verify the functionality of memristors in those applications. Therefore, it may be inferred that there is no one memristor model that fulfils all objectives.

## 3.3 Related Work

Memristors have been used extensively in both analogue and digital applications. The recent developments for memristors in analogue domains include memory circuit for artificial intelligence and machine learning \[[45](#ch03.xhtml#r3_45),[45](#ch03.xhtml#r3_45)\], memristive biosensors \[[46](#ch03.xhtml#r3_46)\], artificial neural networks \[[44](#ch03.xhtml#r3_44),[47](#ch03.xhtml#r3_47),[48](#ch03.xhtml#r3_48)\], neuromorphic applications \[[35](#ch03.xhtml#r3_35),[44](#ch03.xhtml#r3_44),[49](#ch03.xhtml#r3_49),[50](#ch03.xhtml#r3_50)\], speech encryption \[[43](#ch03.xhtml#r3_43)\], spiking neural network \[[30](#ch03.xhtml#r3_30),[44](#ch03.xhtml#r3_44)\], convolutional neural network \[[41](#ch03.xhtml#r3_41)\], associative memories \[[51](#ch03.xhtml#r3_51)\], memory cell design \[[7](#ch03.xhtml#r3_7),[9](#ch03.xhtml#r3_9),[52](#ch03.xhtml#r3_52), [53](#ch03.xhtml#r3_53), [54](#ch03.xhtml#r3_54), [55](#ch03.xhtml#r3_55), [56](#ch03.xhtml#r3_56)\], and physical unclonable function (PUF) \[[2](#ch03.xhtml#r3_2),[45](#ch03.xhtml#r3_45),[57](#ch03.xhtml#r3_57), [58](#ch03.xhtml#r3_58), [59](#ch03.xhtml#r3_59)\].

Recent developments of memristor-based circuits in digital domain are random number generator \[[60](#ch03.xhtml#r3_60)\], ternary memory application \[[61](#ch03.xhtml#r3_61)\], in-memory computation \[[62](#ch03.xhtml#r3_62)\], ternary memristor decode, logic gates \[[63](#ch03.xhtml#r3_63),[64](#ch03.xhtml#r3_64)\], flip flop, Advanced Encryption Standard (AES) encryption \[[44](#ch03.xhtml#r3_44),[65](#ch03.xhtml#r3_65)\], and key generation for encryption \[[44](#ch03.xhtml#r3_44),[66](#ch03.xhtml#r3_66)\].

[Figure 3.3](#ch03.xhtml#f3_3) depicts the investigation conducted on the research done using memristors in both the domains. For using memristors, it is necessary to have a model that accurately replicates the behaviour of a memristor, in addition to providing mathematical models, circuit designs, and simulation tests. Since 2008, many Simulation Program with Integrated Circuit Emphasis (SPICE) models and emulators of memristors have been developed to facilitate the design and simulation of memristor circuits. Although there have been some advancements in SPICE models and practical emulators, the progress in developing a digital circuit simulator for memristors is falling behind. Nevertheless, the accessibility of simulation tools poses a challenge when designing a comprehensive memristor-based system. [Figure 3.4](#ch03.xhtml#f3_4) depicts the various software applications utilized for simulating circuits based on memristors.

![FIGURE 3.3 Memristor research domains.](images/Fig03_03.jpg)

[FIGURE 3.3](#ch03.xhtml#f3_3b) Memristor research domains.

![FIGURE 3.4 Software used for memristor-based circuit design.](images/Fig03_04.jpg)

[FIGURE 3.4](#ch03.xhtml#f3_4b) Software used for memristor-based circuit design.

Memristors have a lot of potential for use in a wide range of applications, and looking into its basic nature, it is an obvious choice to be used for memory design. Various memory circuits, such as 1R, 1T1R, 2T2R, 2T2M, 3T2R, 5CNTFET2R, 6T1M, 7T1R, 7T2M, 8T2R, and 9T2R, are designed and compared in [Table 3.2](#ch03.xhtml#t3_2).

[TABLE 3.2](#ch03.xhtml#t3_2b)Comparison of Memristor-based Memory Circuits

  
| Memory Cell || Number of Access Transistors || Number of Data Storage Elements || Circuit Design Issues |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 6T SRAM cell \[[67](#ch03.xhtml#r3_67)\] || Two transistors || Four transistors || Affected by process, voltage, and temperature (PVT) variations and increased read and write delays |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 2T2M \[[7](#ch03.xhtml#r3_7)\] || Two transistors in the form of transmission gate || Two memristors connected in differential configuration || Immune to PVT variation during read operation |
| 2T2M \[[68](#ch03.xhtml#r3_68)\] || Two transistors || Two memristors connected in differential configuration || Increased write delay |
| 1T1M \[[8](#ch03.xhtml#r3_8)\] || One transistor || One memristor connected in single-ended configuration || 

1.  Optimization of reading voltage, pulse width, height, and speed for correct read operation
2.  For writing, high voltage is required to achieve fast switching

 |
| 5CNFET2M \[[53](#ch03.xhtml#r3_53)\] || Four carbon nano tube field effect transistors (CNTFET) in the form of transmission gates || Two memristors connected in differential configuration || Increased area |
| 6T1M \[[69](#ch03.xhtml#r3_69)\] || Two along with four control transistors || One memristor || Increased leakage power during read operation |
| 7T2M \[[54](#ch03.xhtml#r3_54)\] || Two transistors || Four transistors along with two control memristors and one control transistor || Increased area |

Referring to all the above circuits, the memory cells are majorly evaluated for read delay, write delay, hold state power consumption, and static noise margin. Thus, there remains a scope for a better memory cell design using a memristor.

## 3.4 Design Consideration for Memristor-Based Memory Design

Referring to all the above circuit designs, for memory circuits as shown in [Table 3.2](#ch03.xhtml#t3_2), the parameters evaluated are read delay, write delay, hold power, and area. For an efficient memory design using a memristor, the desirable parameters which need to be considered \[[70](#ch03.xhtml#r3_70), [71](#ch03.xhtml#r3_71), [72](#ch03.xhtml#r3_72)\] are shown in [Table 3.3](#ch03.xhtml#t3_3).

[TABLE 3.3](#ch03.xhtml#t3_3b)Design Consideration for a Memory

 
| Sr. No || Parameters || Desired Value || Typical Value |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1. || $\\frac{R\_{\\text{OFF}}}{R\_{\\text{ON}}}\\text{ ratio}$ || As high as possible || Around 200 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 2. || Endurance (Years) || As high as possible || \>10 |
| 3. || Data retention (s) || As high as possible || 10⁵s |
| 4. || Device speed || As high as possible || – |
| 5. || Sneak path current || As low as possible || – |
| 6. || Data refreshment (number of cycles) || As high as possible || ~ 14 × 10³ |
| 7. || Read delay (ns) || As low as possible || Few ns |
| 8. || Write delay (ns) || As low as possible || < 50 ns |
| 9. || Area (μm²) || As low as possible || – |

An increased $\\frac{R\_{\\text{OFF}}}{R\_{\\text{ON}}}$ ratio is advantageous for facilitating a correct read operation. However, during the read operation, state drift occurs in the memristor, necessitating memory refreshing. When designing, it is important to evaluate the trade-off between speed and robustness, taking into account the state drift issue.

Endurance is measured by the frequency of write operations on a device without causing any impact on the stored data, and it is intended that this value should be maximized.

During the read mechanism, state drift occurs which can read wrong data. Thus, a high data retention rate is desirable. One of the ways to achieve this is using bidirectional storage mechanism \[[69](#ch03.xhtml#r3_69)\], which will disconnect the circuit from the bit lines during read operation. Data refreshment circuit also needs to be designed to overcome the erroneous value due to the state drift condition. One way to accomplish this is to design multiple programs/erase circuit, and the second way is to design a circuit such that the read access time is less than the switching rate of the memristor \[[53](#ch03.xhtml#r3_53)\].

Additionally, the memory circuit design considerations also need to be considered. This basically involves the selection of a memory architecture, the design of read and write operation mechanisms, the choice of a sensing mechanism, and the selection of suitable peripheral circuitry for connecting with memristor arrays.

The memory architectural options include crossbar arrays and grid Resistive Random Access Memory (RRAM) architecture \[[56](#ch03.xhtml#r3_56)\]. The grid RRAM architecture exhibits higher energy efficiency and superior access time. Nevertheless, its style exhibits larger dimensions, resulting in increased expenses. As no access transistors are required for designing the memory cell using the crossbar structure, this architecture is quite appealing as it occupies a compact area. While the crossbar RRAM topology offers advantages in terms of compactness, it also presents challenges in switching time, access time, and reliability \[[56](#ch03.xhtml#r3_56)\]. Furthermore, it is crucial to establish a proficient and efficient read and write mechanism for the cell, ensuring optimal performance in terms of efficiency, speed, and reliability. This entails taking into account many parameters, such as voltage and current levels, pulse lengths, and sensing mechanisms.

## 3.5 Simulation of the Memory Cell using Memristors

The operations and architecture of 2T2M RRAM have been described in this section. As shown in [Figure 3.5](#ch03.xhtml#f3_5), the circuit uses transmission gates comprising of transistors M1 (PMOS) and M2 (NMOS) as compared to the traditionally used NMOS access transistor. The transmission gate allows for both strong one and strong zero logic without any signal degradation. The memristors M3 and M4 act as the storage devices and are connected in differential configuration so that it is less susceptible to PVT variations as opposed to single-ended connection. The linear ionic drift model using the Joglekar window function is employed for the memristor, which addresses the challenges associated with nonlinear dopant drift. The simulation is conducted using LTspice® software, an open-source simulator that is compatible with SPICE and permits both netlist programming and waveform analysis. Memristor parameters selected are *R*[ON] = 100*Ω*, *R*[OFF] = 16K*Ω*, *R*[INIT] = 11K*Ω*, *D* = 10, *p* = 10.

![FIGURE 3.5 2T2M memory cell.](images/Fig03_05.jpg)

[FIGURE 3.5](#ch03.xhtml#f3_5b) 2T2M memory cell.

### 3.5.1 For Write Operation

For storing any bit in a cell, the word line (WL) should be connected to logic high and word line bar (WLB) to a logic low. The data to be stored is applied at data line (DL), and the complement of applied data is provided to bit line (BL) and bit line bar (BLB). As the memristors are connected in a differential configuration, either of the memristor will be ON, and the data will be stored at the storage node. The output of the write ‘1’ operation is illustrated in [Figure 3.6](#ch03.xhtml#f3_6).

![FIGURE 3.6 Output for write operation.](images/Fig03_06.jpg)

[FIGURE 3.6](#ch03.xhtml#f3_6b) Output for write operation.

In order to read the stored data value, BL and BLB must be discharged prior to the read operation. WL is then given a logic high, followed by WLB, receiving a logic low. As soon as the transistors of the transmission gate become active, BL and BLB begin to charge at a rate proportional to their resistance levels. The data will be sensed by the sense amplifier when the voltage difference exceeds 50 mV.

## 3.6 Results and Discussion

For simulating our proposed device, firstly, the HP Lab’s linear drift model of the memristor was implemented. Every Metal Oxide Semiconductor Field Effect Transistor (MOSFET) component was simulated utilizing a 45-nm technology node. The results obtained for write delay using the proposed RRAM cell is compared with the other memory circuits. The write delay of a memristor in the proposed circuit is determined by averaging the write delays of write-1 and write-0. The write latency for the proposed 2T2M is 0.39 μs. As shown in [Table 3.4](#ch03.xhtml#t3_4), the write delay of this circuit is greater than the other memory cell designs described in Ref. \[[7](#ch03.xhtml#r3_7)\]. This is contributed to the fact that the linear ionic drift model used in the memristor assumes the following three things: a homogeneous electric field, average ion mobility, and a linear connection between applied voltage and current, and hence, it is not a typical indication of memristive features. The technology node and the operating voltages are different for each memory cell. This design is a demonstration of the framework suggested for an efficient memory design cell. Hence, when designing any memristor-based memory circuit, careful selection of parameters for optimum performance should be targeted.

[TABLE 3.4](#ch03.xhtml#t3_4b)Comparison of Write Delay

 
| Memory cell || 1R \[[55](#ch03.xhtml#r3_55)\] || 1T1R \[[52](#ch03.xhtml#r3_52)\] || 2T2M \[[68](#ch03.xhtml#r3_68)\] || 2T2M \[[7](#ch03.xhtml#r3_7)\] || 5CNTFET2M \[[9](#ch03.xhtml#r3_9)\] || 6T1M \[[69](#ch03.xhtml#r3_69)\] || 7T2M \[[54](#ch03.xhtml#r3_54)\] || Proposed circuit |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Write delay || – || 100 ns || 1 ns || 25 ns || 2.4 ns || 3.3 ns || 8.66 ps || 0.39 μs |

## 3.7 Conclusion

Memristors are a special kind of electrical components that are used in neuromorphic systems, logic circuits, and memory, among other things. Memristive technologies are appealing because of their nonvolatility, scalability, and compatibility with CMOS. To effectively construct and mimic memory based on memristors, a variety of memristor models have been proposed. The goal of this examination is to make researchers aware of the basic framework that needs to be followed when designing a memristor-based memory cell along with its design parameters. As there isn’t a single memristor model that fits all applications, researchers have difficulties when trying to properly simulate and evaluate memristor-based memory circuits. The designed 2T2M memory cell suffers from increased read and write delays as compared to its counterparts. Thus, it is concluded that appropriately selecting a model which will fit your need is a major task. It will take some time before memristor-based circuits are deployed in real-world applications.

# 4 Innovative Design and Optimization of High-Power AmplifiersA Comparative Study with GaN HEMT and CMOS Technologies

Manju Yadav, Ramji Gupta, Abhishek Narayan Tripathi, and Alpana Pandey

DOI: [10.1201/9781003483038-4](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003483038-4)

## 4.1 Introduction

Power amplifier (PA) is an electrical device that boosts the strength or amplitude of an electrical signal. It’s an important part of communication systems, audio systems, and many other applications where a signal has to be amplified to a greater power level \[[1](#ch04.xhtml#r4_1), [2](#ch04.xhtml#r4_2), [3](#ch04.xhtml#r4_3)\].

A PA’s main job is to take in a low-power electrical signal and boost it up to a level where it can power a load, like a motor, a loudspeaker, or an antenna. It retains the authenticity and quality of the original signal while taking a small input signal and producing a bigger, more powerful output signal \[[4](#ch04.xhtml#r4_4)\]. PAs are classified into different types based on the amplification technology used. Some common types include:

1.  **Transistor Amplifiers:** Because of their great performance, small size, and efficiency, transistor-based amplifiers are utilized extensively. They can be divided further into field-effect transistor (FET) amplifiers and bipolar junction transistor (BJT) amplifiers \[[5](#ch04.xhtml#r4_5)\].
2.  **Vacuum Tube Amplifiers:** Due to their distinct sound qualities, vacuum tubes, including triodes, tetrodes, and pentodes, were a common component of early amplifiers and are still preferred by certain audiophiles \[[6](#ch04.xhtml#r4_6)\].
3.  **Operational Amplifiers, or Op-Amps**, are integrated circuits made expressly to increase the gain and precision of signals. They can be coupled with power stages to form PAs, even though their primary use is in low-power applications.
4.  **Class-D Amplifiers:** To get high efficiency, Class-D amplifiers employ pulse-width modulation (PWM) techniques. They are frequently utilized in audio systems, particularly in conjunction with portable speakers and subwoofers \[[7](#ch04.xhtml#r4_7)\].

PA design factors include regulating heat dissipation, choosing the right amplification technology, figuring out the necessary power output, assuring stability, and safeguarding against overloads and short circuits \[[8](#ch04.xhtml#r4_8),[9](#ch04.xhtml#r4_9)\].

In many electronic systems, PAs are essential for providing crystal clear audio \[[10](#ch04.xhtml#r4_10)\], dependable wireless connectivity, and effective power transfer. With the development of technology, their design principles and performance characteristics have continued to change, improving power delivery and amplification for a variety of applications \[[11](#ch04.xhtml#r4_11),[12](#ch04.xhtml#r4_12)\].

The first section of the review covers the fundamental functions and features of PAs, emphasizing important variables, including o/p (output) power, gain, and efficiency. In addition, this chapter examines different approaches which are used in the development of PAs, including GaN HEMT and CMOS technologies. The related work of PAs is discussed in the second section. Further, a comparative analysis is conducted between GaN HEMT and CMOS technologies for wireless communication applications, focusing on the fundamentals of power amplifiers and evaluating the performance differences between these technologies. In the last section, conclusion is discussed.

## 4.2 Related Work

Tuffy and Neal developed a distributed real frequency technique synthesis algorithm for wideband operation, achieving over 70% efficiency over a 51% bandwidth. The algorithm was linearized using digital predistortion \[[13](#ch04.xhtml#r4_13)\]. Using a 2-mm GaN-Si die, Tuffy, Neal, and Lyndon Pattison created a small, hybrid PA, which is class J and its operating frequency range is 1–2.1 GHz. The power output of the PA was 13–18 W, and its drain efficiencies ranged from 60% to 69% \[[14](#ch04.xhtml#r4_14)\]. The work by Chen, Peng, Merrick, and Brazil contrasts electromagnetic-based and circuit-based Beamforming Operation (BO) for creating 10 W and 30 W Pas, and the output power is 39.8 dBm. Circuit-based BO attains a drain efficiency of over 60%, whereas Electromagnetic EM-based BO provides an output power of 43.8 dBm and 57% drain efficiency \[[15](#ch04.xhtml#r4_15)\]. Pang and Jingzhou discuss the need for broadband and multiband PA design techniques for multiband/multi-mode equipment, using GaN HEMT CGH40010F as an active device and performing load-pull simulations to find optimal impedances \[[16](#ch04.xhtml#r4_16)\].

Dong, Yitian, Yi Huang, and Ling Qiu propose a joint optimization of the transmit covariance matrix and active antenna set to maximize energy efficiency in Multi-User Multiple Input Multiple Output (MU–MIMO) systems with nonideal PAs \[[17](#ch04.xhtml#r4_17)\]. Chen and Peng, propose a multiobjective Bayesian optimization method for broadband Differential Power Analysis (DPA) design, utilizing EM simulation for more accurate performance and minimizing mismatch between desired and simulated impedance trajectories \[[8](#ch04.xhtml#r4_8)\]. You, Fei, and Johannes Benedikt \[[12](#ch04.xhtml#r4_12)\] propose a methodology for designing broadband PAs using optimized-load-impedance calculation, I–V curve mining, direct impedance calculation, waveform regulations, and harmonic balance simulations for accurate Zopt solutions. The technique allows for simple, compact multiband matching networks without affecting network architecture or frequency bands. A triple-band amplifier was designed using this method, achieving maximum power-added efficiencies and output powers \[[18](#ch04.xhtml#r4_18)\]. Kouhalvandi, Lida, Osman Ceylan, and Serdar Ozoguz talk about an automated process and approach for creating a high-power amplifier (PA) that is optimized. The authors also note that automated RF circuit design tools are gaining popularity among researchers and provide efficient resolutions in analog and radio frequency (RF) system designs \[[19](#ch04.xhtml#r4_19)\].

The authors, Li and Chuan, describe an enhanced SA-PSO approach to automate the design of high-efficiency PAs. The choice of starting values is crucial for Electronic Design Automation (EDA) optimization, and because PAs have nonlinear features and a large number of parameters, most initial values end up in the local optimization and cause the Harmonic Balance (HB) simulation to converge slowly \[[20](#ch04.xhtml#r4_20)\]. For linearizing RF PAs in wideband communication systems, Le and Duc Han, propose an adaptive Iterative Learning Algorithm (ILA) linearization utilizing the Reduced-Parameter Empirical Model (RPEM) method, which outperforms Least Mean Squares (LMS) or Recursive Least Squares (RLS) and successfully compensates for nonlinear distortion under various circumstances \[[21](#ch04.xhtml#r4_21)\]. Zhang, Yumeng, and Bruno Clerckx suggest a channel-adaptive waveform design strategy to maximize transmitter input waveforms while taking energy harvester and high-PA nonlinearities into account. They do this by reformulating the structure of the PA and applying Barrier’s method \[[22](#ch04.xhtml#r4_22)\].

Zhang, Lei, Kaixue Ma, and Haipeng Fu provide a 60-GHz PA by using 55-nm CMOS for high power. They attain good flatness between the frequency range 57 GHz and 62 GHz \[[23](#ch04.xhtml#r4_23)\]. Guixiang Jin discusses the design and implementation of an output transformer for a power amplifier, focusing particularly on its filtering capabilities to reduce harmonic distortion and enhance the transformer’s overall efficiency \[[24](#ch04.xhtml#r4_24)\]. In order to maximize bias voltages, Rawat and Arvind Singh suggest a 919–923 MHz CMOS PA for LoRa applications \[[25](#ch04.xhtml#r4_25)\].

## 4.3 Fundamentals of Power Amplifier

PAs are electronic devices that boost the power of electrical signals \[[26](#ch04.xhtml#r4_26)\], essential in audio, wireless communication, radar applications \[[27](#ch04.xhtml#r4_27)\], and RF transmitter applications. Nowadays, PA design is done with the optimization techniques for different applications like Bayesian optimization, optimized-load-impedance calculation, particle swarm optimization, waveform optimization for wireless power transfer with PA, and optimization of cascode structure. \[[28](#ch04.xhtml#r4_28),[29](#ch04.xhtml#r4_29)\].

Fundamentally, PAs have the following key characteristics:

1.  **Power Output:** A PA’s power output is its maximum RF output power, determining its ability to deliver desired signal strength or coverage in specific applications \[[30](#ch04.xhtml#r4_30)\].
    
    *P*[out] = *I*²*R*[out](4.1)
    
    Measured in watts or decibels relative to a reference power, it is crucial for signal strength. The power output of a PA depends on various factors \[[31](#ch04.xhtml#r4_31)\]. PAs’ power output is influenced by several factors, including supply voltage, load impedance, linearity, operating frequency, and thermal considerations \[[32](#ch04.xhtml#r4_32)\]. Supply voltage increases power output, while load impedance matches the amplifier to the load. Linearity allows for higher output without distortion \[[33](#ch04.xhtml#r4_33)\]. Operating frequency affects power output, and thermal management ensures reliable operation at higher power levels.
    
    PA output is the maximum achievable power under ideal conditions, but practical factors like power supply, thermal constraints, and device limitations can lower the actual output \[[34](#ch04.xhtml#r4_34)\]. Selecting the right amplifier is crucial.
    
2.  **Power Gain:** PAs increase power levels by amplifying low-power input signals. The gain, the ratio between output and input power, is determined by the amplifier’s design and specifications \[[35](#ch04.xhtml#r4_35)\]. It can be fixed or adjustable, with some having a manufacturer-determined gain setting or variable gain control for system requirements \[[36](#ch04.xhtml#r4_36)\]. There are two ways to express gain:
    
    1.  **Voltage Gain**: The ratio of output voltage \[[37](#ch04.xhtml#r4_37)\] to input voltage and is typically expressed by the formula in equation (4.2):
        
        *A*[*V*] = (*V*[OUT]/*V*[IN])(4.2)
        
    2.  **Power Gain (Ap)**: The relation between the output to input provides gain. It is represented by equation (4.3):
        
        *A*[*P*] = 10 \* log[10](*P*[OUT]/*P*[IN])(4.3)
        
        The gain of a PA is influenced by input/output impedance matching, load conditions, and design parameters. So, accurate determination requires considering these factors and amplifier specifications \[[38](#ch04.xhtml#r4_38)\].
        
3.  **Efficiency:** Efficiency is how much output power is received from the input power \[[39](#ch04.xhtml#r4_39)\]. The efficiency is represented by equation (4.4):
    
    $\\text{Eff}.\\left( \\eta \\right) = \\frac{\\text{Output\\ power}}{\\text{Input\\ power}}\*100\\%$(4.4)
    
    It is usually expressed as a percentage.
    
    Power-added efficiency (PAE)
    
    Power-added efficiency (PAE) basically converts DC input power into RF output power \[[40](#ch04.xhtml#r4_40),[41](#ch04.xhtml#r4_41)\]. The PAE formula is given by equation (4.5):
    
    $\\text{PAE} = \\frac{\\text{RF\\ output\\ power} - \\text{RF\\ input\\ power}}{\\text{DC\\ input\\ power}}\*100\\%$(4.5)
    

### 4.3.1 S-Parameters

The S-parameters are scattering parameters, which are useful for describing PAs. They describe the impedance, the gain, the bandwidth, and the stability \[[42](#ch04.xhtml#r4_42)\].

S11 (or S22): In PA, S11 describes about the power reflected back at either the input port or the output port because of an impedance mismatch. The S22 describes the stability factor, which is referred to as the Rollett stability factor; a value of less than 1 indicates stability \[[43](#ch04.xhtml#r4_43)\]. S21 (or S12): The higher values of the S21 indicate more power amplification, while the reverse gain is represented by S12.

That’s why the S-parameters for PAs are important for PA design prediction, analysis of the parameters, input and output impedance matching, stability, and performance prediction for precise frequency \[[44](#ch04.xhtml#r4_44),[45](#ch04.xhtml#r4_45)\].

## 4.4 Discussions on Future Aspects and Challenges of Power Amplifiers

For designing the PA, we are using transistors and different CMOS (complementary metal-oxide-semiconductor) to provide high gain and high efficiency for different applications \[[46](#ch04.xhtml#r4_46),[47](#ch04.xhtml#r4_47)\]. Some general techniques for designing PAs are discussed in this section.

### 4.4.1 Design of Power Amplifier Circuit with Different Technologies and Details about Parameters with Graphs

#### 4.4.1.1 Using GaN HEMT Power Amplifier

Gallium Nitride (GaN) high-electron-mobility transistor (HEMT) technology provides a wide-bandgap semiconductor substantial with special power handling abilities and also a high breakdown voltage ability \[[48](#ch04.xhtml#r4_48)\]. By comparing GaN HEMT \[[49](#ch04.xhtml#r4_49)\] PA with conservative technologies such as silicon-based Metal-Oxide-Semiconductor Field-Effect Transistors (MOSFETs) or GaAs-based HEMTs, the GaN HEMT PAs provide advantages of high efficiency, low power, and high gain for high-frequency operations \[[50](#ch04.xhtml#r4_50),[51](#ch04.xhtml#r4_51)\].

[Figure 4.1](#ch04.xhtml#f4_1) shows the block diagram of the PA, in which the first block includes an input matching network with an inductor and a capacitor components. The second block represents the technology used like MOSFET, transistor, and GaN HEMT, and the third block shows the output matching network with a capacitor and an inductor component. This block diagram represents how input power amplifies the input signal by using an input/output matching network with appropriate component values of *L*, *C*, *R*, *V*[gs], and *V*[ds].

![FIGURE 4.1 Block diagram of power amplifier.](images/Fig04_01.jpg)

[FIGURE 4.1](#ch04.xhtml#f4_1b) Block diagram of power amplifier.

[Figure 4.2](#ch04.xhtml#f4_2) represents the GaN HEMT PA circuit, which is implemented on LT Spice software with appropriate values of the *L*, *R*, *C*, *V*[ds], and *V*[gs.]

![FIGURE 4.2 GaN HEMT power amplifier circuit design \[19\].](images/Fig04_02.jpg)

[FIGURE 4.2](#ch04.xhtml#f4_2b) GaN HEMT power amplifier circuit design \[[19](#ch04.xhtml#r4_19)\].

In this simulation, the GaN HEMT EPC 2007 model is used for the verification.

[Figure 4.3](#ch04.xhtml#f4_3) represents the output power of the GaN HEMT PA, which shows the output power variation between the frequency range of 1.7–2.5 GHz. The output power starts increasing from a frequency of 1.5 GHz and gets a maximum value of 49 dB at 2.12 GHz. Due to the S11 parameter (return loss), the output power exponentially decreases beyond the frequency of 2.14 GHz.

![FIGURE 4.3 Output power of GaN HEMT power amplifier.](images/Fig04_03.jpg)

[FIGURE 4.3](#ch04.xhtml#f4_3b) Output power of GaN HEMT power amplifier.

Because of return loss (S11), the gain of the PA decreases exponentially beyond the 2.12 GHz frequency shown in [Figure 4.4](#ch04.xhtml#f4_4), which also starts increasing from the frequency 1.5 GHz and gets a maximum value of 11 dB at 2.12 GHz, after which the frequency gain again decreases up to 2.5 GHz.

![FIGURE 4.4 GaN HEMT power amplifier gain.](images/Fig04_04.jpg)

[FIGURE 4.4](#ch04.xhtml#f4_4b) GaN HEMT power amplifier gain.

[Figure 4.5](#ch04.xhtml#f4_5) represents the S11 (return loss) parameter of the GaN HEMT PA. Its value decreases between the frequency range 2.00–2.22 GHz and a minimum −5dB at frequency 2.14 GHz. Because of this, the high power output is achieved at 2.14 GHz.

![FIGURE 4.5 GaN HEMT power amplifier S11 parameter.](images/Fig04_05.jpg)

[FIGURE 4.5](#ch04.xhtml#f4_5b) GaN HEMT power amplifier S11 parameter.

[Figure 4.6](#ch04.xhtml#f4_6) represents the PAE of GaN HEMT is 49%–86% for the frequency range 1.5–2.5 GHz, and the maximum value is 86% at frequency 2.12 GHz.

![FIGURE 4.6 GaN HEMT power-added efficiency.](images/Fig04_06.jpg)

[FIGURE 4.6](#ch04.xhtml#f4_6b) GaN HEMT power-added efficiency.

[Figure 4.7](#ch04.xhtml#f4_7) represents the S21 parameter (transmission coefficient/forward gain), which starts increasing from frequency 1.7–2.12 GHz, and the maximum value obtained −6 dB at 2.12 GHz; so at this frequency, forward gain is high; after this frequency, it starts decreasing exponentially up to 2.3 GHz.

![FIGURE 4.7 GaN HEMT power amplifier S21 parameter.](images/Fig04_07.jpg)

#### 4.4.1.2 CMOS 45 nm Power Amplifier \[[52](#ch04.xhtml#r4_52)\]

The CMOS 45 nm PA uses a 45-nm technology to amplify the PA signals \[[53](#ch04.xhtml#r4_53),[54](#ch04.xhtml#r4_54)\]. There are various applications of the CMOS 45 nm PAs. Basically, CMOS PAs are used for wireless communication systems \[[55](#ch04.xhtml#r4_55)\]. They are used to increase the strength of the signal which is transmitted to the antenna. They are also used in various audio devices to increase the strength of the audio signals, and examples are headphones or speakers \[[56](#ch04.xhtml#r4_56),[57](#ch04.xhtml#r4_57)\]. We choose one circuit design \[[19](#ch04.xhtml#r4_19)\] and provide different technologies to get the gain, power output, and PAE and find the best technology GaN HEMT for further research purposes. Also, [Table 4.1](#ch04.xhtml#t4_1) provides an analysis of the PAE of different technologies. The paper \[19\] presents GaN HEMT technology with a maximum PAE of 77%, while the simulated results of the technology discussed here show a higher PAE of 86%. This demonstrates that the simulated GaN HEMT technology outperforms the results in paper \[19\] in terms of power-added efficiency (PAE).

The maximum gain of 34.8 dB was achieved using 100 nm GaN HEMT technology where the PAE was 2.6 dB and output power delivered was 20.3 dB. ## 4.5 Conclusion

The PA is the most important device used in electronics and communication engineering. There are various applications of PAs using CMOS 45 nm, CMOS 22 nm, and GaN HEMT technologies. The wireless communications and radar systems mostly use GaN HEMT’s PA for high power density, high-frequency operation, and to get high efficiency. The various technologies provide different outputs for gain, efficiency, and power output. The best result in terms of PAE is found by using GaN HEMT transistors. The power level, the frequency range, the efficiency, the cost, and the integration are some of the applications that provide the selection of PA technology. The GaN HEMT PA is best suited for high-power and high-frequency applications, whereas the CMOS PA technologies provide various application-specific approaches.
