# 14 Transforming ElectronicsAn Extensive Analysis of Hyper-FET Technological Developments and Utilisation

Debashish Dash and Manwendra Chauhan

DOI: [10.1201/9781003483038-14](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003483038-14)

## 14.1 Introduction

The development of transistors, the essential components of contemporary electronics, has been fuelled by the semiconductor industry’s unrelenting march towards miniaturisation. Every generation of transistors, starting from the modest bipolar junction transistor (BJT) and ending with the widely used metal-oxide-semiconductor field-effect transistor (MOSFET), has improved performance and efficiency, accelerating technological progress in a variety of disciplines. However, as conventional MOSFET scaling approaches its limits, the search for a new transistor architecture that can maintain Moore’s Law has been more intense. The hyper-FET is a cutting-edge competitor that has the potential to completely change the transistor market. Unlike the planar construction of MOSFETs, hyper-FETs claim a gate-all-around (GAA) architecture, where the channel is fully encompassed by the gate electrode \[[1](#ch14.xhtml#r14_1), [2](#ch14.xhtml#r14_2), [3](#ch14.xhtml#r14_3), [4](#ch14.xhtml#r14_4)\]. This creative design provides several significant benefits.

**Decreased Gate Tunnelling**: Leakage currents are reduced by the close contact between the gate and channel, which lowers power consumption and boosts energy efficiency.

**Improved Mobility**: Higher carrier mobility and quicker device operation are made possible by the GAA structure’s greater electrostatic control over the channel.

**Better Scalability**: By reducing the channel length without sacrificing gate control, the GAA shape pushes the limits of transistor density \[[5](#ch14.xhtml#r14_5),[6](#ch14.xhtml#r14_6)\].

These inherent advantages have propelled hyper-FETs to the forefront of transistor research, attracting significant attention from both academia and industry \[[7](#ch14.xhtml#r14_7), [8](#ch14.xhtml#r14_8), [9](#ch14.xhtml#r14_9)\]. Numerous research groups worldwide are actively exploring the potential of this technology, and the field is witnessing rapid advancements in material engineering, device fabrication, and circuit design.

This chapter explores the fascinating field of hyper-FETs, carefully analysing their basic ideas, features, and state-of-the-art at the moment \[[10](#ch14.xhtml#r14_10)\]. We set out on a quest through the complex field of study, examining significant discoveries, recognising pressing issues, and considering viable remedies. We also illuminated how new device designs and developing materials will play a critical role in determining the direction of hyper-FET technology in the future ([Figure 14.1](#ch14.xhtml#f14_1)).

![FIGURE 14.1 Hyper-FET structure.](images/Fig14_01.jpg)

[FIGURE 14.1](#ch14.xhtml#f14_1b) Hyper-FET structure.

## 14.2 Hyper-FET Devices and Applications

The search for efficient, high-performance transistors for next-generation electronics has prompted the investigation of novel materials and structures. Among these fascinating competitors is the hyper-FET, a new transistor with a GAA design and the tantalising prospect of vanadium dioxide (VO2) integration \[[11](#ch14.xhtml#r14_11), [12](#ch14.xhtml#r14_12), [13](#ch14.xhtml#r14_13), [14](#ch14.xhtml#r14_14), [15](#ch14.xhtml#r14_15)\].

Of the various ways to improve the performance of hyper-FETs, one intriguing path involves the use of vanadium dioxide (VO2) \[[16](#ch14.xhtml#r14_16)\]. This material is interesting because it shows an amazing insulator-to-metal phase transition that can be induced by electrical or thermal stimulation. Through the intentional integration of VO2 into hyper-FET architectures, scientists are opening new and fascinating avenues for ultra-low-power electronics.

In its insulating condition, VO2 functions as a dynamic gate, essentially turning off current via the hyper-FET channel while in standby mode. This significantly reduces leakage currents and static power consumption, which are a major bottleneck in conventional transistors. By introducing a voltage or heat pulse, VO2 quickly transitions to its metallic phase, restoring conductivity and allowing active device functioning. This breakthrough method provides the path for new circuit designs with near-zero standby power, significantly increasing overall energy efficiency.

The integration of VO2 presents its own set of challenges, requiring precise control over material properties and device fabrication techniques. However, the potential rewards are substantial, pushing the boundaries of low-power electronics and opening doors for applications in battery-powered devices, mobile computing, and the ever-expanding internet of things (IoT) ([Figure 14.2](#ch14.xhtml#f14_2)).

![FIGURE 14.2 Schematic of the hyper-FET \[40\].](images/Fig14_02.jpg)

[FIGURE 14.2](#ch14.xhtml#f14_2b) Schematic of the hyper-FET \[[40](#ch14.xhtml#r14_40)\].

Near 68°C, this unusual material transitions from an insulator to a metallic state. Below this temperature, its I–V curve mimics that of an insulator, with very little current flow. Above the transition, the curve becomes linear, indicating metallic behaviour and higher conductivity.

The distinct I–V properties of phase transition materials have applications in a variety of sectors, including memory devices: Hysteretic behaviour can be utilised to produce non-volatile memories, such as memristors, whose resistance state is determined by the voltage history \[[17](#ch14.xhtml#r14_17)\].

**Sensors**: The sensitivity of conductivity to temperature, pressure, or magnetic field can be used for sensing purposes.

**Electronic Switches**: The rapid change in conductivity during the phase transition can be used to switch and control devices. Understanding the I–V properties of phase transition materials allows us to realise their enormous promise for novel technology and intriguing future applications ([Figure 14.3](#ch14.xhtml#f14_3)).

![FIGURE 14.3 I-V curve of the PTM device \[40\].](images/Fig14_03.jpg)

[FIGURE 14.3](#ch14.xhtml#f14_3b) I-V curve of the PTM device \[[40](#ch14.xhtml#r14_40)\].

This complicated dance of electrons and phases opens the way for revolutionary circuit designs, promising ultra-low-power operation is ideal for wearables, IoT devices, and mobile computing \[[18](#ch14.xhtml#r14_18), [19](#ch14.xhtml#r14_19), [20](#ch14.xhtml#r14_20)\]. Hyper-FETs help to make devices more environmentally friendly by reducing energy usage.

**Faster Data Processing**: Increased transistor speed enables powerful, efficient computing and communication systems.

## 14.3 Power and Speed Evaluation of Hyper-FETs

Hyper-FETs, with their GAA architecture and improved electron control, are promising candidates for the next generation of transistors \[[21](#ch14.xhtml#r14_21), [22](#ch14.xhtml#r14_22), [23](#ch14.xhtml#r14_23)\]. However, the pursuit of quicker, more efficient electronics necessitates a careful examination of their power and speed capabilities. This section digs deeply into the methodology and criteria used to assess these essential features of hyper-FETs, providing a thorough analysis.

However, before they can dominate the circuits, we must first grasp their power consumption. This requires analysing three essential metrics:

**DC Power Consumption**: This shows how much power the hyper-FET consumes while idle (leakage) and during switching. Minimising both is critical to efficiency.

**Leakage Current**: This cunning culprit keeps the current flowing even while the hyper-FET is cooling. Hyper-FETs, with their improved gate control, significantly minimise this when compared to ordinary MOSFETs.

**Energy Efficiency**: This indicates how much energy is consumed by each flip of the hyper-FET switch, measured in Joules per operation. The lower the number, the more effective the device ([Figure 14.4](#ch14.xhtml#f14_4)).

![](images/Fig14_04a.jpg)![FIGURE 14.4 (a) Voltage transfer characteristic of a hyper-FET inverter (schematic also shown) \[41\]. (b) Waveforms corresponding to a hyper-FET inverter with the internal states of the n-type and p-type hyper-FETs \[41\].](images/Fig14_04b.jpg)

[FIGURE 14.4](#ch14.xhtml#f14_4b) (a) Voltage transfer characteristic of a hyper-FET inverter (schematic also shown) \[[41](#ch14.xhtml#r14_41)\]. (b) Waveforms corresponding to a hyper-FET inverter with the internal states of the n-type and p-type hyper-FETs \[[41](#ch14.xhtml#r14_41)\].

To accurately assess these variables, we use a three-pronged attack:

**Device Level**: We directly measure voltage and current on the hyper-FET, peeking inside like a microscopic detective.

**Circuit Level**: We place the hyper-FET in real-world circuit simulations, observing how it reacts under various workloads and determining its practical power consumption.

**Benchmarking**: We compare the hyper-FET to known champions such as FinFETs, using power measurements to objectively analyse efficiency advances. By using these methods, we can unravel the secrets of hyper-FET power, paving the path for a future in which transistors operate harder but not on our batteries. To determine the full speed potential of hyper-FETs, researchers use a set of critical metrics and evaluation methods. Switching delay \[[24](#ch14.xhtml#r14_24)\] is an important metric that reveals how long it takes for the hyper-FET to gracefully flip between logic states, much like a dancer changing positions. Minimising this delay is critical for increasing circuit speed and facilitating faster processing. Cut-off frequency (fT), a metric of the hyper-FET’s high-frequency capability, denotes the frequency at which its current gain gracefully drops to unity. A higher fT, like a musician’s broader vocal range, indicates faster signal amplification and opens possibilities for high-speed applications. Propagation delay estimates how long it takes for a signal to travel through a single stage of a multi-stage hyper-FET circuit, like a relay race in which each stage must pass the baton quickly. Reducing this delay increases the overall circuit speed and ensures a smooth flow of information. To evaluate these metrics, researchers use a variety of techniques. Electrical measurements require time-domain characterisation via pulsed pulses, like a stopwatch measuring a sprinter’s split timings \[[25](#ch14.xhtml#r14_25),[26](#ch14.xhtml#r14_26)\]. This directly evaluates switching delays and other transient responses, demonstrating the hyper-FET’s agility. Frequency-domain analysis examines the hyper-FET’s gain and phase response, like mapping a dancer’s choreography onto a musical score. This examination reveals its high-frequency performance and cut-off frequency, demonstrating its ability to execute graceful manoeuvres at high speeds. Circuit simulations provide a virtual stage for testing hyper-FET-based circuits under a variety of conditions, much like a dress rehearsal before the big show. Observing propagation delays and total speed across diverse applications demonstrates their versatility and adaptation to different choreographies. Understanding hyper-FET performance necessitates recognising the complex link between power and speed. Optimising one frequently impacts the other, creating a fascinating dance of trade-offs: high speed, high power: aggressive voltage scaling for faster switching results in increased dynamic power usage. Balancing speed needs with adequate voltage scaling is critical for optimal performance ([Figures 14.5](#ch14.xhtml#f14_5) and [14.6](#ch14.xhtml#f14_6)).

![FIGURE 14.5 Delay versus PTM transition time for hyper-FET inverter \[41\].](images/Fig14_05.jpg)

[FIGURE 14.5](#ch14.xhtml#f14_5b) Delay versus PTM transition time for hyper-FET inverter \[[41](#ch14.xhtml#r14_41)\].

![](images/Fig14_06a.jpg)![FIGURE 14.6 Power ratio versus frequency for inverter chain (a) periodic input and (b) random input \[41\].](images/Fig14_06b.jpg)

[FIGURE 14.6](#ch14.xhtml#f14_6b) Power ratio versus frequency for inverter chain (a) periodic input and (b) random input \[[41](#ch14.xhtml#r14_41)\].

**Material Selection**: Materials with higher mobility have lower leakage currents, perhaps resulting in a better power-speed trade-off. Exploring innovative materials is critical to achieving more optimisation \[[27](#ch14.xhtml#r14_27), [28](#ch14.xhtml#r14_28), [29](#ch14.xhtml#r14_29)\].

**Circuit Design**: Tailoring circuit design to individual application requirements can improve power-speed balance. Techniques like logic families and power gating can provide considerable advantages. Evaluating hyper-FETs primarily based on power and speed measurements provides an incomplete picture \[[30](#ch14.xhtml#r14_30)\]. Real-world performance requires a more holistic approach, considering additional factors.

**Fabrication Complexity** \[[31](#ch14.xhtml#r14_31),[32](#ch14.xhtml#r14_32)\]: The manufacturing issues connected with GAA \[[33](#ch14.xhtml#r14_33)\] structures might have an impact on scalability and cost, affecting their overall viability.

**Integration Challenges**: To ensure seamless integration into real-world systems, compatibility with existing circuits and design processes must be carefully considered.

**Emerging Technologies**: Integrating innovative materials such as vanadium dioxide for dynamic threshold control has the potential to boost power efficiency even more \[[34](#ch14.xhtml#r14_34), [35](#ch14.xhtml#r14_35), [36](#ch14.xhtml#r14_36), [37](#ch14.xhtml#r14_37), [38](#ch14.xhtml#r14_38), [39](#ch14.xhtml#r14_39)\].

## 14.4 Conclusion

Hyper-FETs are on the verge of revolutionising the world of electronics. Their GAA architecture and improved electrostatics point to a future of faster, more efficient transistors capable of catapulting computers to new heights and altering areas ranging from mobile devices to renewable energy technology.

This chapter has explored the complex environment of hyper-FET research, focusing on power and speed characteristics, integration issues, and prospective applications. While various challenges remain, such as fabrication complexity and material restrictions, the research community is persistently seeking novel solutions. Novel materials, such as vanadium dioxide, hold great promise for additional efficiency improvements, while advances in circuit design and manufacturing techniques pave the way for more seamless integration with existing systems.

As we conclude this chapter, the enthusiasm for hyper-FETs is obvious. Their dance of force and speed is rife with possibilities, and the potential rewards are apparent. While complete acceptance of this disruptive technology may take time, the seeds of change have been planted, and the future of electronics is undeniably bright with the promise of hyper-FETs.

# 15 VLSI Realization of Smart Systems Using Blockchain and Fog Computing

R. Hanumantharaju, K. N. Shreenath, Sagarika Choudhury, Swagata Devi, and Naushad Manzoor Laskar

DOI: [10.1201/9781003483038-15](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003483038-15)

## 15.1 Introduction

The recent development of technology is having an impact on every aspect of human existence and altering how we utilize and view the world. Similar to the improvements the technology has brought about in other spheres of life, it is likewise discovering new avenues for advancement in the healthcare industry.

The progress of embedded systems has been upward-sloping during the last few decades. Indeed, a variety of industries have seen the use of embedded systems. The significance of innovative technology for human health and well-being has led to an expansion in its application across several domains in recent times. The use of artificial intelligence algorithms to evaluate data from multiple sensors is actually a useful new technique for detecting anomalies, diseases, problems, or alerts. Actually, it paves the way for the development of an effective medical diagnostic assistance system based on embedded systems.

The enhancement of security, the user experience, and the other aspects of the healthcare industry are the primary advantages that come with technological innovation. Electronic medical record (EMR) and electronic health record (EHR) systems provide these advantages. They still have certain problems with data integrity, user ownership, security of medical information, and so on. Blockchain technology could be the new technology that solves these problems. This technology promises to give medical records and other healthcare-related data a safe, uncheckable platform to be stored on. Before the development of contemporary technology, the medical field stored medical records utilizing handwritten mechanisms on paper-based systems. This system of medical records on paper was unorganized, insecure, inefficient, and susceptible to tampering. Because the patient’s medical records were duplicated and redundant in every institution the patient visited, it also had to deal with this problem. There was a shift in the healthcare industry toward EHR systems, which integrated paper-based records and EMRs. Clinical notes and laboratory results were stored in these systems’ various components. It was suggested that they improve patient safety by reducing errors and facilitating easier access to information. EHR systems were designed to address the issues with paper-based medical records and offer a productive solution that would revolutionize the healthcare industry. Many hospitals throughout the world have adopted EHR systems because of their many advantages, chief among them being increased security and cost-effectiveness. Given that it gives healthcare a lot of functionality, they are regarded as an essential component of the healthcare industry. These features include lab test results, patient appointment scheduling, billing and accounts, and EMR storage. Many of the EHR systems in use in the healthcare industry have them available. Providing safe, tempered-proof, and scalable medical records across various platforms is the main goal. The proposed work aims to clarify data privacy and security issues in smart healthcare systems, analyze patient health records in fog nodes, protect data privacy through blockchain technology, allow authorized access to records, assist businesses in processing registered claims, and help files transfer securely using InterPlanetary File System (IPFS). In order to safely satisfy the needs of medical privacy and secrecy, the blockchain is used. In this work, the heart rate of patients is collected using smart figure sensors. Gathered by an embedded platform, this encrypted data will be processed on a backend server before being sent on, functioning as a clever data relay.

## 15.2 Literature Survey

The survey primarily focuses on the advantages of blockchain and its applications for improving the security and privacy of EHRs. It also examines problems with the current EHR system and how to fix them with cutting-edge technologies like blockchain. Shahnaz et al. \[[1](#ch15.xhtml#r15_1)\] elucidate a system that could be used to implement blockchain innovation for EHRs in the medical services sector. Our proposed system’s primary goal is to implement blockchain technology for EHRs. It also aims to provide secure electronic record storage by defining granular access rules that are specific to the system’s users. According to Jetley and Zhang \[[2](#ch15.xhtml#r15_2)\], the growing adoption of EHR as a supplemental information source in both Information Systems (IS) and supporting fields is a result of the medical services associations’ increased acceptance of EHR frameworks. According to Cowie et al. \[[3](#ch15.xhtml#r15_3)\], EHR frameworks are gradually storing imaging data that provide a more accurate picture of infection than is currently available in the majority of observational data sets. In \[4\] some countries, the EHR can be connected, on an individual basis, to various informational indices, such as general public well-being and lifestyle studies, disease vaults, and data collected in various areas. According to Mayer et al. \[[4](#ch15.xhtml#r15_4)\], a comprehensive literature review on EHRs within a blockchain was carried out to identify and debate the primary problems, obstacles, and potential advantages of blockchain adoption in the healthcare industry. Zheng et al. \[[5](#ch15.xhtml#r15_5)\] describe an outline of blockchain design and some commonplace agreement calculations utilized in various blockchain. Moreover, specialized difficulties and late advances are briefly recorded, additionally spreading out conceivable future patterns for blockchain. This chapter centers on blockchain innovation rather than advanced monetary forms. The importance of interoperability in EHRs for the smooth exchange of data among various healthcare stakeholders is discussed in the work done by Sonkamble et al.. \[[6](#ch15.xhtml#r15_6)\]. According to Milojkovic \[[7](#ch15.xhtml#r15_7)\], the blockchain functions as an appropriate record and is an attached information structure. To do this, all of the data on the blockchain must be recreated for each hub in the framework \[[8](#ch15.xhtml#r15_8)\]. In comparison to current systems, a decentralized EHR system was proposed in this chapter, offering improved security and effective data sharing. The systematic analysis of existing blockchain-based solutions for safeguarding clinical data independent of distributed computing is described by Mahajan et al. \[[9](#ch15.xhtml#r15_9)\]. The review focused on incomplete studies published in the last few years regarding blockchain-based security and identified various gaps and difficulties in the analysis. An architecture with the user, fog, and cloud tiers is proposed by Hanumantharaju et al. \[[10](#ch15.xhtml#r15_10)\]. According to Zīle and Strazdiņa \[[11](#ch15.xhtml#r15_11)\] who reviewed the literature, this chapter’s goal is to provide a vague synopsis of the blockchain use cases that are currently being used in the data innovation sector. In addition, this chapter provides an overview of the core innovative perspectives and operational principles of blockchain technology, enabling an assessment of the presented use cases. An architecture with the user, fog, and cloud tiers is proposed by Hanumantharaju et al. \[[12](#ch15.xhtml#r15_12)\]. Lakhan et al. \[[13](#ch15.xhtml#r15_13)\] elucidate how each medical clinic haze hub is directly connected to the remote cloud. Fatokun et al. \[[14](#ch15.xhtml#r15_14)\] describe the demonstration of how blockchain technology can be used to improve EHR systems and how the problems with privacy, security, and data exchange can be resolved by their suggested model.

According to a recent projection \[[15](#ch15.xhtml#r15_15)\], the blockchain is recognized in the healthcare sector as a helpful tool to save costs, enhance the quality of medical records, and avoid data breaches. Healthcare practitioners are adopting internet of things (IoT) and modernizing the analysis and care process. The internet has made it possible to connect billions of sensors and devices. In patient care and therapy, the technology for remote patient monitoring is now commonly employed. These tools do, however, also pose serious privacy and security risks when it comes to data transfer and transaction logging. Security and confidentiality problems pertaining to medical information could potentially result in treatment delays or even endanger the patient’s life. Blockchain is used to securely manage and evaluate sensitive medical data \[[16](#ch15.xhtml#r15_16)\]. IoT architecture is discussed in the article by Dammak et al. \[[17](#ch15.xhtml#r15_17)\] in order to monitor personal healthcare data. For the purpose of gathering Electrocardiogram (ECG) data, they employed an Arduino board equipped with a sensor. In order to expedite proof-of-work consensus, it was proposed in the article by Frikha et al. \[[18](#ch15.xhtml#r15_18)\] to leverage an embedded architecture to generate a Hardware Intellectual Property (HW IP) in order to improve system efficiency, particularly with regard to real-time and low-power limits. A Verilog IP is employed by Tien et al. \[[19](#ch15.xhtml#r15_19)\] with field programmable gate arrays (FPGAs).

## 15.3 Design and Implementation

This section provides a thorough overview and insight into the potential design of our suggested solution by discussing the architecture that was employed and the initial user interface that was conceived. [Figure 15.1](#ch15.xhtml#f15_1) shows the overall architectural design of the system. Admin can be a government entity or an authorized organization. Only the administrator can add the participants (hospital, doctor, insurance company, and patient) to the blockchain. Hospitals further can add doctors to their network. Doctors can view the previous records of patients only when a patient has given access to a particular doctor; the doctors cannot make any changes to existing data in the records but can add information to it. Patients can register themselves with insurance companies or the hospitals can also register patients with insurance companies. Insurance companies have access to patients’ data (view only). Patients can also add their records directly or using the fog node (block) and add them to IPFS storage. The patient can only view his/her records.

![FIGURE 15.1 Architecture design.](images/Fig15_01.jpg)

[FIGURE 15.1](#ch15.xhtml#f15_1b) Architecture design.

### 15.3.1 VLSI Hardware-Based Design

A heterogeneous embedded platform is used to create a multi-input EHR that takes advantage of numerous sensors connected via the IoT. The permissioned Ethereum blockchain, which this application employs, double-secures the data. In this endeavor, the goal is to apply a secure algorithm which ensures health information protection. It is imperative that confidential information will be difficult to obtain or readily compromised. The literature illustrates the energy-intensive efficiency of blockchain technology. Strong Personal Computer (PCs), servers, and Graphics Processing Unit (GPUs) are therefore used. We provide a system that is faster, more secure, energy-efficient, and uses fewer resources than a conventional PC. Energy conservation should also be handled by the hardware and software working together. With the use of FPGA, a fast system is the one that is safe and low power. Various types of sensors with a variety of data are employed. These medical data are obtained from a variety of sources, such as healthcare facilities and paramedical patient data (heart rate). These specifics are either immediately used in transactions or stored in a database. The database link is used to protect transactions after it has been encrypted.

### 15.3.2 Proposed System

Wearable technology is used to collect data, such as heart rate. When the wearable system option is chosen, a smartphone serves as a relay to send data to “our system” via Bluetooth or Wi-Fi, and an embedded hardware with a powerful multiprocessor is used. Every bit of information that embedded hardware collects is stored locally in a database. Stored data is accessed via a web server that runs on the integrated hardware, making it simple and straightforward for a variety of users to access. Blockchain is used to store vast amount of data. Ethereum blockchain technology will make use of an embedded hardware design. The components are as follows ([Figure 15.2](#ch15.xhtml#f15_2)):

![FIGURE 15.2 Block diagram of proposed system.](images/Fig15_02.jpg)

[FIGURE 15.2](#ch15.xhtml#f15_2b) Block diagram of proposed system.

1.  Different nodes that facilitate the formation of encrypted transactions are operated by the embedded hardware, which is connected via Wi-Fi. Before being delivered, the data is encrypted and transferred via a connection to the FPGA.
2.  The complex encryption and the blockchain mining functions are controlled by the FPGA chip. Using FPGA as a cryptoprocessing unit instead of embedded hardware-based software, encryption has one additional important advantage. With FPGA, data storage security can be increased. First off, the architecture can be greatly customized using specific Central Processing Unit (CPU) cores that are neither extensively used nor challenging to decode. The second step involves using encrypted bitstreams, which fortifies the decryption procedure even further.

### 15.3.3 Tools for Software

React is a front-end JavaScript library that is free and open-source and is used to create user interfaces using User Interface (UI) components. Meta and a group of independent developers and businesses maintain it.

**Android Studio**: The official Integrated Development Environment for creating Android applications is called Android Studio. It offers additional features that boost app developers’ productivity. Its primary component is a Gradle-based build system that supports NDK and C++ and offers a quick and feature-rich emulator for app testing.

**Flutter**: With just one codebase, developers can create stunning natively compiled desktop, web, and mobile applications with Flutter, Google’s portable UI toolkit. Flutter is a free and open-source software that integrates with existing code and is utilized by developers and organizations globally.

Ethereum virtual machine is a computation engine with millions of executable projects that functions similarly to a decentralized computer. With Remix, you can write Solidity contracts directly from your browser. It’s an open-source tool.

Truffle, Ganache, and Drizzle are the three distinct tools that make up the Suite ecosystem for Web3 development. Truffle is a framework for testing smart contracts, an asset pipeline, and a development environment.

Ethereum is an open-source, decentralized blockchain that supports smart contracts. Ethereum is a decentralized blockchain platform that creates a peer-to-peer network for the safe execution and validation of smart contract application code. Without the need for a reliable central authority, participants can transact with one another using smart contracts. The proof-of-work consensus, which is currently used by the Ethereum blockchain, is carried out by miners competing to create new blocks that contain processed transactions. The victor receives some newly created Electronic Health Record (ETH) in addition to sharing the new block with the entire network. The computer that can complete a math problem the fastest wins the race and creates the cryptographic link between the current block and the previous block. It is the task of “proof-of-work” to solve this puzzle. The proof-of-work consensus mechanism, which requires more than 51% of the votes to approve a transaction, protects the network.

Visual Studio is an open-source software that is both lightweight and powerful, and it requires an extension to be developed further.

MetaMask is a free online multipurpose cryptocurrency wallet that lets users interact with the Ethereum blockchain, store and exchange virtual currencies, and host an evolving collection of decentralized applications (dApps).

Ganache is a personal Ethereum blockchain where you can develop apps, deploy contracts, run tests, and carry out other tasks all for free. It is also used to test smart contracts. To test your Solidity contracts, you can set up a personal Ethereum blockchain using Ganache. It is used to test smart contracts. The Ganache provides the local environment for the development of the blockchain which creates an emulated Ethereum network hosted over a local network, and it provides the Fake Ethereum for carrying out the transaction with actual cost calculation for each transaction which can be customized and can be directly pushed into the main Ethereum network after complete development.

**NPM Node JS**: Written entirely in JavaScript, Node Package Manager (NPM) is the default package manager for Node.js. NPM is a command-line client that manages all of the Node.js packages and modules. Along with Node.js installation, it is installed on the system. NPM is used to install the necessary packages and modules for the Node project. All the files required for a module are included in a package, and modules are JavaScript libraries that can be included in a Node project based on the project’s specifications. The package.json file is not able to install all of a project’s dependencies using NPM. Packages can also be updated and uninstalled. Using the semantic versioning scheme, each dependency in the package.json file can specify a range of acceptable versions, enabling developers to automatically update their packages without having to make unintentional breaking changes. An open-source development platform called Node.js (Node) is used to run JavaScript scripts server side. Node is helpful when creating apps that need a continuous connection between the browser and the server. Real-time apps like chat, news feeds, and web push notifications are common uses for Node. Node.js is designed to use a single thread with a single process at a time and to operate on a dedicated HTTP server. Applications built with Node.js are asynchronous and event based. On the Node platform, the code is not written in the conventional sequence of receive, process, send, wait, and receive. Rather, Node sends out short requests one after the other without waiting for a response, handling incoming requests in a continuous event stack.

### 15.3.5 Algorithms

Consensus methods in blockchain platforms can be classified as permissioned (such as Hyper Ledger and Ethereum Private) or permissionless (such as Ethereum). A permissioned blockchain has preselected nodes, as opposed to a permissionless blockchain where anyone can join. This makes it possible to use highly scalable and bandwidth-efficient consensus types. Proof-of-authority (PoA) consensus is a type of consensus that offers fault tolerance and high performance. Gavin Wood, a cofounder of Ethereum and Parity Technologies, coined the term in 2017.

**Working of PoA**: In PoA, nodes that have demonstrated their legitimacy are granted the ability to create new blocks. These nodes are known as “validators,” and the software they run enables them to group transactions into blocks. Since the process is automated, validators do not need to keep a close eye on their computers, but they do need to keep them secure. PoA works well in distributed trust environments, such as public networks like PoA Network. Because the PoA consensus algorithm values identities, block validators are staking their reputations rather than coins. PoA is protected by confidence in the chosen identities.

**Conditions for PoA Consensus**: PoA consensus is generally applied under the following conditions, though it may vary depending on the specific implementation. Validators must attest to their true identities. A candidate needs to be prepared to risk both his reputation and money. Strict selection procedures lower the possibility of choosing dubious validators and encourage sustained dedication to the blockchain. Every candidate must be treated equally in the validator selection process. Verification of validators’ identities is necessary to preserve the blockchain’s integrity. There ought to be a procedure in place to choose trustworthy validators.

**Advantages of PoA Consensus**: High capacity for risk new blocks that are generated at a predictable interval that varies for PoW, provided that 51% of the nodes are not acting maliciously. **High Transaction Rate**: This is more robust than computationally intensive algorithms like PoW.

**Limitations**: PoA is an attempt to improve the efficiency of centralized systems; PoA validators are publicly visible. It is not decentralized. The identities of the validators could be compromised by outsider manipulation.

### 15.3.6 Module Implementations

**IPFS MODULE**: Peer-to-peer, or P2P, storage networks are what IPFS is. Peers can access content from anywhere in the globe by relaying, storing, or doing both at once. If you ask IPFS to locate something, it can do so by using its content address instead of its location. Understanding IPFS is based on three key concepts: content discovery using distributed hash tables (DHTs), content linking via directed acyclic graphs (DAGs), and unique identification via content addressing. The IPFS ecosystem is made possible by these three pillars that support one another. Let us begin by discussing content addressing and content unique identification. To store patient medical records, doctor-consulted reports, and other data, we have implemented an IPFS module. Physicians, insurance companies, and patients can all access the same records.

**Transaction Module**: We handled the transactions using a software cryptocurrency wallet called MetaMask, which communicates with the Ethereum blockchain. Through a mobile app or browser extension, it enables users to access their Ethereum wallet, which can be used to interact with decentralized applications. This module’s implementation includes the MetaMask wallet, which keeps track of several user accounts and their corresponding cryptocurrency balances. Every transaction made through this wallet is mined by Ganache. Additionally, the transaction is completed using the money in the wallet. Starting with the front end, we used the JavaScript Library ReactJS and combined it with some styling libraries Tailwind CSS and Bootstrap. We implemented it using a template of boilerplate code generated by Truffle Suite which has the predefined connectivity with the Ethereum blockchain via Web3.js. We used a few tools to implement the blockchain as a backend: Ganache, which offers a private Ethereum blockchain environment that lets you emulate the Ethereum blockchain to interact with smart contracts, limited accounts, and cryptocurrency testing; and Solidity, an object-oriented programming language. We handled the transactions using a software cryptocurrency wallet called MetaMask, which communicates with the Ethereum blockchain. Through a mobile app or browser extension, it enables users to access their Ethereum wallet, which can be used to interact with decentralized applications. The majority of healthcare organizations today lack the infrastructure necessary to safeguard patient data from unauthorized access, which means that current EHRs might not satisfy patients’ privacy demands. Healthcare 4.0, which makes use of blockchain, cloud computing, big data, and the IoT, has forced the industry to address security issues with processing medical data. To secure such data, several centralized cryptography solutions were introduced; however, they fell short of fully resolving the issues.

## 15.4 Results

[Figure 15.4](#ch15.xhtml#f15_4) describes the admin page. Admin is the trusted center of authority; any participant in the network has to be approved by the Admin. Before making any transaction in the network, the participant must be added by the Admin. [Figure 15.5](#ch15.xhtml#f15_5) shows the Patient Dashboard where the patient has the option to grant or revoke access to viewing/adding his/her data onto the blockchain. [Figure 15.6](#ch15.xhtml#f15_6) shows the patient’s home page where the patient can view the previous medical history and descriptions given by the doctors and also can add data to the existing blockchain. [Figure 15.7](#ch15.xhtml#f15_7) shows the doctor’s home page where the doctor can view the patient’s previous records. Also, a doctor has the option to add records to the patient’s existing data. The doctor also has the option to add the consultations for the patient’s future reference. When patient records are accessed in an EHR system, all such events must be documented in a log file for future access history audits. The log file can be viewed as a legal document and is used to reconstruct the previous state of medical records. As a result, we should, if at all possible, make the log file unchangeable and strictly guard against unauthorized access. They employ a centralized system that makes it possible for physicians and patients to look up a patient’s dispersed medical records. One disadvantage of such a centralized architecture is that as the system gets bigger, it may run the risk of becoming a single point of failure and developing a data flow bottleneck. Patients fill out the information. The patient’s information, including their heart rate, will be computed and entered into the patient list. The date of the patient’s checkout will also be stored. Patient checkup (test) files can be kept in this patient list. Both the patient and the doctor have access to it. Additionally, the doctor can add files and consult with patients in the patient list. The doctor will consult with the patient during the additional consultation. In the patient list, they can also upload files. [Figure 15.8](#ch15.xhtml#f15_8) shows the appointments of the doctor, i.e., the patients who have given their access to add records to their block.

## 15.5 Conclusion

This work implemented a practical e-health platform based on VLSI systems and a proof of concept for blockchain-based e-health system. Since many hospitals and medical patients are linked through EHRs, robust security-based cryptography algorithms that are computationally efficient should be necessary to form the blockchain model. When performing medical data sharing, the blockchain model should take into account the possibility of various threats, including quantum threats. Scalability, computational load, and security performance efficiency are the three main requirements of the blockchain model. The model ought to have a low computation and a low power overhead. Our work also focuses on patients who will visit several hospitals in their lifetime. Since each hospital will have its own EHR and structure, it will be difficult to transfer EHR within the same hospital, which will lead to loss of lots of patient data that could be used to analyze a patient’s health history for better medication and health. Considering a current methodology where each hospital stores patient data in its central database, which poses a risk of a single point of failure, and which can be prevented by using a distributed structure of blockchain, the data is securely distributed across multiple nodes, ensuring redundancy and resilience. In future work, we intend to integrate a variety of health monitoring tools and enhance performance. Additionally, we intend to incorporate the payment module into the current framework. To do this, we must take a few factors into account. Specifically, we must determine the cost of a patient’s doctor consultation on this decentralized blockchain system. To adhere to the values of the healthcare industry, we would also need to define specific policies and regulations. Millions of people’s EHRs could be stored in an EHR. With time, the volume of data will increase exponentially. For blockchain, a scalable system is required. The additional overhead brought on by the bandwidth resources needed to mine a new block and the broadcast communication to every network node is another issue with the suggested system. Dynamic reconfiguration could be added to this work to further improve IP usage and energy consumption.
